     def write(self, data: dict[str, dict[str, Any]]):
-        # Move the cursor to the beginning of the file just in case
-        self._handle.seek(0)
+        if self._handle.closed:
+            raise ValueError('I/O operation on closed file.')
+
+        if not self._writable:
+            raise IOError('Cannot write to the database. Access mode is "{0}"'.format(self._mode))
 
         # Serialize the database state using the user-provided arguments
         serialized = json.dumps(data, **self.kwargs)
 
-        # Write the serialized data to the file
-        try:
-            self._handle.write(serialized)
-        except io.UnsupportedOperation:
-            raise IOError('Cannot write to the database. Access mode is "{0}"'.format(self._mode))
+        # Instead of overwriting the database file in place, write the data
+        # to a temporary file and then move it into place. Moving a file is
+        # atomic, so the database file never ends up half-written, even if
+        # the process dies or the disk runs full while writing. The temporary
+        # file has to be in the same directory, as files can only be moved
+        # atomically within the same file system.
+        directory, filename = os.path.split(self._path)
+        fd, temp_path = tempfile.mkstemp(

+        try:
+            with open(fd, self._write_mode, encoding=self._encoding) as temp:
+                temp.write(serialized)
+
+                # Ensure the data has been written to disk before the file
+                # replaces the database file
+                temp.flush()
+                os.fsync(temp.fileno())
+
+            # Temporary files are only accessible by the current user, so
+            # keep the permissions of the database file (if possible)
+            try:
+                mode = stat.S_IMODE(os.stat(self._path).st_mode)
+                os.chmod(temp_path, mode)
+            except OSError:
+                pass
+
+            # Windows can't replace files that are open, so close the handle
+            # first and re-open the file afterwards
+            self._handle.close()
+            try:
+                os.replace(temp_path, self._path)
+            finally:
+                self._handle = self._open_for_reading()
+        except BaseException:
+            # Don't leave the temporary file behind
+            try:
+                os.remove(temp_path)
+            except OSError:
+                pass
+
+            raise
+
+        # Make sure the file's replacement is persisted, too
+        _fsync_directory(directory)
+
+    def _reopen_if_replaced(self) -> None:
+        """
+        Re-open the database file if it has been replaced since opening it.
 
-        # Remove data that is behind the new cursor in case the file has
-        # gotten shorter
-        self._handle.truncate()
+        This happens if another storage instance writes to the same file, as
+        writing replaces the file. Our handle would then keep reading the old
+        version.
+        """
+        try:
+            current = os.stat(self._path)
+        except OSError:
+            # The file has been removed, so keep reading what we've got
+            return
+
+        if not os.path.samestat(os.fstat(self._handle.fileno()), current):
+            handle = self._open_for_reading()
+            self._handle.close()
+            self._handle = handle
+
+    def _open_for_reading(self) -> IO[Any]:
+        return open(self._path, mode=self._read_mode, encoding=self._encoding)

+- Fix: Make ``JSONStorage`` writes atomic. The data is now written to a

+  file, so the database is no longer corrupted when the process dies or the

     user-controlled values for callable arguments (e.g. ``default`` or
     ``cls``) as they are executed in-process on every write operation.
 
+.. note::
+    ``JSONStorage`` writes atomically: it writes the data to a temporary file
+    in the same directory and then replaces the database file with it. So the
+    database file always contains either the old or the new data, even if the
+    process dies or the disk runs full while writing. If a write fails, the
+    temporary file is removed right away. If the process is killed while
+    writing, its temporary file (``.<file name>.<random>.tinydb.tmp``) is
+    removed the next time the database is opened for writing. This requires
+    the directory containing the database file to be writable. On Windows, a
+    file can't be replaced while another program or ``TinyDB`` instance has it
+    open, so writing fails in that case.
+
 To modify the default storage for all ``TinyDB`` instances, set the
 ``default_storage_class`` class variable:

+@pytest.mark.parametrize('failing_function', ['fsync', 'replace'])
+def test_json_failed_write_keeps_old_data(tmpdir, monkeypatch,
+                                          failing_function):
+    """
+    If a write fails midway (e.g. because the disk is full), the database
+    file has to keep its previous contents
+    """
+    path = str(tmpdir.join('test.db'))
+    old_data = {'_default': {str(i): {'value': 'x' * 50} for i in range(20)}}
+    new_data = {'_default': {}}
+
+    storage = JSONStorage(path)
+    storage.write(old_data)
+
+    with open(path, 'rb') as handle:
+        old_contents = handle.read()
+    old_stat = os.stat(path)
+
+    def fail(*args):
+        raise OSError(errno.ENOSPC, 'No space left on device')
+
+    with monkeypatch.context() as m:
+        m.setattr(os, failing_function, fail)
+
+        with pytest.raises(OSError, match='No space left on device'):
+            storage.write(new_data)
+

+    with open(path, 'rb') as handle:
+        assert handle.read() == old_contents
+    assert os.path.samestat(os.stat(path), old_stat)
+    assert os.stat(path).st_mtime_ns == old_stat.st_mtime_ns
+    assert storage.read() == old_data
+
+    # ...no temporary file has been left behind...
+    assert os.listdir(str(tmpdir)) == ['test.db']
+
+    # ...and the storage can still be used
+    storage.write(new_data)
+    assert storage.read() == new_data
+    assert os.listdir(str(tmpdir)) == ['test.db']
+    storage.close()
+

+@pytest.mark.parametrize('encoding, kwargs', [
+    ('utf-8', {'indent': 4, 'sort_keys': True}),
+    ('utf-16', {'ensure_ascii': False}),
+    ('cp936', {'ensure_ascii': False, 'indent': 2, 'separators': (',', ': ')}),
+])
+def test_json_read_after_write(tmpdir, encoding, kwargs):
+    """
+    Reads see what has just been written, the file contains exactly what
+    ``json.dumps`` returns for the given options (in the given encoding) and
+    no temporary files are left behind
+    """
+    path = str(tmpdir.join('test.db'))
+    storage = JSONStorage(path, encoding=encoding, **kwargs)
+
+    for i in [3, 10, 0, 7, 1]:
+        data = {'_default': {str(n): {'text': u'こんにちは世界', 'n': n}
+                             for n in range(i)}}
+        storage.write(data)
+
+        assert storage.read() == data
+
+        with open(path, 'rb') as handle:
+            serialized = json.dumps(data, **kwargs)
+            expected = serialized.replace('\n', os.linesep).encode(encoding)
+            assert handle.read() == expected
+
+        assert os.listdir(str(tmpdir)) == ['test.db']
+
+    storage.close()

+def test_json_read_after_failed_reopen(tmpdir, monkeypatch):
+    """
+    If re-opening the file after replacing it fails, the write has still
+    succeeded and the next read re-opens the file

+
+    def fail_once():
+        calls.append(None)
+        if len(calls) == 1:
+            raise OSError(errno.EMFILE, 'Too many open files')
+        return open_for_reading()
+
+    monkeypatch.setattr(storage, '_open_for_reading', fail_once)
+
+    storage.write(doc)
+    assert storage.read() == doc
+    assert len(calls) == 2
+    storage.close()

+def test_json_removes_temp_files_of_interrupted_writes(tmpdir):
+    path = str(tmpdir.join('test.db'))
+    touch(path, create_dirs=False)
+
+    leftover = tmpdir.join('.test.db.k3j2_9ab.tinydb.tmp')
+    leftover.write('{"_default": {"1": ')
+
+    # Files that only look similar are kept, like the temporary files of the
+    # database "test.db.v2"
+    unrelated = ['.test.db.backup', '.test.db.v2.k3j2_9ab.tinydb.tmp',
+                 'test.db.k3j2_9ab.tinydb.tmp', 'other.json']
+    for name in unrelated:
+        tmpdir.join(name).write('')
+
+    # Opening the database read-only doesn't remove anything
+    JSONStorage(path, access_mode='r').close()
+    assert leftover.exists()
+
+    JSONStorage(path).close()
+    assert not leftover.exists()
+    assert sorted(os.listdir(str(tmpdir))) == sorted(unrelated + ['test.db'])
+

+    itself as failed.
+
+    :param path: The directory to flush.
+    """
+    try:
+        fd = os.open(path, os.O_RDONLY)
+    except OSError:
+        return
+
+    try:
+        os.fsync(fd)
+    except OSError:
+        pass
+    finally:
+        os.close(fd)

In inflection, titleize mangles acronyms: titleize('NASA rocket launch') returns 'Nasa Rocket Launch' and titleize('read the HTTP spec') returns 'Read The Http Spec'. Can you make titleize keep all-caps acronyms like NASA and HTTP as they are?

Code from the repo can't be run directly on this machine; use the two wrapper scripts, which run it in an isolated container: /home/user/cs_workspaces/CS-03/run_tests.sh [pytest args] (e.g. run_tests.sh -q tests) and /home/user/cs_workspaces/CS-03/run_python.sh -c '...'. Please keep all your work inside /home/user/cs_workspaces/CS-03.


CS-03-session/L000056.00 scripted_user
The coordinator sent a message while you were working:
Thanks. Two refinements: if the whole input is already in uppercase (like a shouted heading 'HELLO WORLD'), titleize should still produce 'Hello World' like it does today - only treat an all-caps word as an acronym when the input also contains lowercase letters. And please don't change the behaviour of humanize() or underscore() themselves; other code relies on them (for example humanize('employee_salary') must stay 'Employee salary', and underscore('NASARocket') must stay exactly what it returns now).

Address this before completing your current task.

     return pluralize(underscore(word))
 
 
+# What titleize() treats as a word when it keeps all-caps words: a run of
+# letters, digits and apostrophes. Combining diacritical marks (U+0300 to
+# U+036F) belong to the word too, because changing the case of a letter can
+# add one ('\u0130'.lower() is 'i' followed by U+0307) and a word must not
+# fall apart just because its case changed.
+_TITLE_WORD_RE = re.compile(r"(?:[^\W_]|['\u0300-\u036f])+")
+
+
+def _is_all_caps(word: str) -> bool:
+    letters = [char for char in word if char.isalpha()]
+    return len(letters) >= 2 and all(char.isupper() for char in letters)
+
+
+def _keep_all_caps(original: str, titled: str) -> str:
+    """
+    Return ``titled``, a word of a title, with the all-caps parts of
+    ``original``, the word it was made from, put back as they were.
+    """
+    if _is_all_caps(original):
+        return original
+    # Otherwise keep only its all-caps parts, like NASA in "NASA's".
+    return "'".join(
+        original_part if _is_all_caps(original_part) else titled_part
+        for original_part, titled_part in zip(
+            original.split("'"), titled.split("'")
+        )
+    )

+def _separate_words(word: str) -> str:
+    """
+    Put underscores between the words of a CamelCased expression and replace
+    dashes with underscores, keeping the case of the letters. This is what
+    :func:`underscore` does before lowercasing.
+    """
+    word = re.sub(r"([A-Z]+)([A-Z][a-z])", r'\1_\2', word)
+    word = re.sub(r"([a-z\d])([A-Z])", r'\1_\2', word)
+    word = word.replace("-", "_")
+    return word

CS-03-eval-alt-2 · valid_alternative · eval · valid — Case-overlay design: titleize keeps its base pipeline verbatim, then (only when the input has lowercase letters) finds the input's all-caps alphanumeric words (apostrophe-joined caps like DON'T count as one) and splices their original spelling back into the base title at positions found by aligning case-folded letters/digits of input and output; humanize/underscore untouched

+    return title
+
+
+def _restore_acronyms(title: str, word: str) -> str:
+    """
+    Put the all-caps words of ``word`` back into ``title``, its titleized
+    form, spelled exactly as they are in ``word``.
+
+    Titleizing keeps the letters and digits of a string in their order; it
+    only changes their case and the separators between them, except that
+    :func:`humanize` drops a trailing ``_id``.  So the n-th letter or digit of
+    ``word`` becomes the n-th letter or digit of ``title``, which tells where
+    each acronym ended up.  The text found there is replaced only when it
+    holds the same letters and digits as the acronym.
+    """
+    word_positions = _alnum_skeleton(word)[1]
+    title_positions = _alnum_skeleton(title)[1]
+    pieces: typing.List[str] = []
+    copied = 0
+    for start, end in _acronym_spans(word):
+        first = bisect.bisect_left(word_positions, start)
+        last = bisect.bisect_left(word_positions, end)
+        if last > len(title_positions):
+            break  # dropped by humanize(), like the "ID" of "user_ID"
+        low = title_positions[first]
+        high = title_positions[last - 1] + 1
+        while high < len(title) and _is_mark(title[high]):
+            high += 1
+        acronym = word[start:end]
+        found = title[low:high]
+        if low >= copied and (
+            _alnum_skeleton(found)[0] == _alnum_skeleton(acronym)[0]
+        ):
+            pieces += [title[copied:low], acronym]
+            copied = high
+    pieces.append(title[copied:])
+    return ''.join(pieces)
+
+
+def _acronym_spans(text: str) -> typing.List[typing.Tuple[int, int]]:
+    """
+    Return the ``(start, end)`` slices of the all-caps words in ``text``.
+
+    A word is a run of letters and digits.  Runs without lowercase letters
+    that are joined by an apostrophe make up a single word, so ``DON'T`` is
+    one all-caps word, and the possessive ``NASA's`` contains the all-caps
+    word ``NASA``.  An all-caps word has at least two uppercase letters and
+    no lowercase ones.
+    """
+    spans: typing.List[typing.Tuple[int, int]] = []
+    for start, end in _word_spans(text):
+        if any(char.islower() for char in text[start:end]):
+            continue
+        if spans and spans[-1][1] == start - 1 and text[start - 1] == "'":
+            start = spans.pop()[0]
+        spans.append((start, end))
+    return [
+        (start, end) for start, end in spans
+        if sum(char.isupper() for char in text[start:end]) >= 2
+    ]
+
+
+def _word_spans(text: str) -> typing.Iterator[typing.Tuple[int, int]]:
+    """
+    Yield the ``(start, end)`` slice of every run of letters and digits in
+    ``text``.  A combining mark belongs to the run of the character it
+    decorates.
+    """
+    start: typing.Optional[int] = None
+    for index, char in enumerate(text):
+        if char.isalnum() or (start is not None and _is_mark(char)):
+            if start is None:
+                start = index
+        elif start is not None:
+            yield start, index
+            start = None
+    if start is not None:
+        yield start, len(text)
+
+
+def _alnum_skeleton(text: str) -> typing.Tuple[str, typing.List[int]]:
+    """
+    Return the case-folded letters and digits of ``text``, leaving out every
+    other character, along with the index in ``text`` that each of them comes
+    from.
+    """
+    letters: typing.List[str] = []
+    positions: typing.List[int] = []
+    for index, char in enumerate(text):
+        for folded in char.casefold():
+            if folded.isalnum():
+                letters.append(folded)
+                positions.append(index)
+    return ''.join(letters), positions
+
+
+def _is_mark(char: str) -> bool:
+    """Tell whether ``char`` is a combining mark, such as an accent."""
+    return unicodedata.category(char).startswith('M')
 
 
 def transliterate(string: str) -> str:

+def _separate_words(word: str) -> str:
+    """
+    Put underscores between the words of a CamelCase or dashed string, like
+    :func:`underscore` does, but keep the case of the letters.
+

+    ``"UR"`` and ``"Ls"``.

+def _is_plural_acronym(word: str) -> bool:
+    """Tell whether ``word`` is an acronym in the plural, like ``"URLs"``."""
+    return len(word) > 2 and word.endswith("s") and word[:-1].isupper()

Could you add a `step` option to more_itertools.consecutive_groups? I want to find runs where items go up by a fixed amount, e.g. consecutive_groups([2, 4, 6, 10, 12], step=2) should give [2, 4, 6] and [10, 12].

The repo is checked out (as a git working copy) at /home/user/cs_workspaces/CS-04/more-itertools. Code from the repo can't be run directly on this machine; use the two wrapper scripts, which run it in an isolated container: /home/user/cs_workspaces/CS-04/run_tests.sh [pytest args] (e.g. run_tests.sh -q tests) and /home/user/cs_workspaces/CS-04/run_python.sh -c '...'. Please keep all your work inside /home/user/cs_workspaces/CS-04.


CS-04-session/L000107.00 scripted_user
The coordinator sent a message while you were working:
Nice. A couple of cases I need too: descending runs with a negative step should work (consecutive_groups([9, 8, 7, 3, 2], step=-1) -> [9, 8, 7], [3, 2]), and step=0 should raise a ValueError rather than doing something weird. Also, existing code calls consecutive_groups(iterable, ordering) with ordering as the second positional argument - that has to keep working, and the groups should still be lazy iterators like before.

Address this before completing your current task.

VariantRequirementKindStatusStatementEvidence


initial_onlyR1changeexplicitmore_itertools.consecutive_groups accepts a new `step` option that can be passed as a keyword argument, e.g. consecutive_groups([2, 4, 6, 10, 12], step=2) is a valid call (no TypeError).“Could you add a `step` option to more_itertools.consecutive_groups?”


initial_onlyR2changeexplicitWith step=k, consecutive_groups splits the input, in order, into maximal runs of adjacent items in which each item is exactly k greater than the previous item (items 'go up by a fixed amount'); a new group starts wherever that does not hold. Example from the user: consecutive_groups([2, 4, 6, 10, 12], step=2) yields exactly two groups, [2, 4, 6] and [10, 12].“I want to find runs where items go up by a fixed amount”; “consecutive_groups([2, 4, 6, 10, 12], step=2) should give [2, 4, 6] and [10, 12]”


initial_onlyR3invariantinferred`step` is optional: calls to consecutive_groups that do not pass `step` keep the existing behavior (grouping runs of items that increase by 1), so existing callers and the existing consecutive_groups tests are unaffected; omitting step behaves like step=1.“add a `step` option to more_itertools.consecutive_groups”

full_sessionR1changeexplicitmore_itertools.consecutive_groups accepts a new `step` option, passed as the keyword argument step=... . When given, it yields groups that are maximal runs of consecutive input items in which each item is exactly `step` more than the item before it; a new group starts wherever the difference from the previous item is not `step`. User example: consecutive_groups([2, 4, 6, 10, 12], step=2) yields the groups [2, 4, 6] and [10, 12].“Could you add a `step` option to more_itertools.consecutive_groups?”; “I want to find runs where items go up by a fixed amount”; “consecutive_groups([2, 4, 6, 10, 12], step=2) should give [2, 4, 6] and [10, 12]”


full_sessionR2changeexplicitA negative step finds descending runs: with step=-k each item in a group is exactly k less than the item before it. User example: consecutive_groups([9, 8, 7, 3, 2], step=-1) yields the groups [9, 8, 7] and [3, 2].“descending runs with a negative step should work (consecutive_groups([9, 8, 7, 3, 2], step=-1) -> [9, 8, 7], [3, 2])”


full_sessionR3changeexplicitPassing step=0 raises ValueError instead of producing any grouping.“step=0 should raise a ValueError rather than doing something weird”


full_sessionR4invariantexplicitBackward compatibility: ordering stays the second positional parameter, so existing calls of the form consecutive_groups(iterable, ordering) (without step) keep working with unchanged results; the new step parameter must not take ordering's positional slot.“existing code calls consecutive_groups(iterable, ordering) with ordering as the second positional argument - that has to keep working”


full_sessionR5invariantexplicitThe groups are still lazy iterators as before: consecutive_groups returns an iterator whose groups are iterators (not lists or tuples) that pull items from the source on demand. This holds for the default step and for a non-default step; the input is not consumed eagerly.“the groups should still be lazy iterators like before”


full_sessionR6invariantinferredstep is optional and defaults to 1: calls without step group runs of items that increase by 1, exactly as before, and step=1 gives the same result as omitting step.“Could you add a `step` option to more_itertools.consecutive_groups?”


full_sessionR7changeinferredstep works together with ordering: when an ordering function is given, an item continues the current group if and only if ordering(item) - ordering(previous item) == step. Example: consecutive_groups('acegjl', ord, step=2) yields ['a', 'c', 'e', 'g'] and ['j', 'l'].“I want to find runs where items go up by a fixed amount”; “existing code calls consecutive_groups(iterable, ordering) with ordering as the second positional argument”

         ['i']
         ['l', 'm', 'n', 'o', 'p']
 
+    By default, *step* is ``1``, so positions must increase by one. Set
+    *step* to find runs with a different spacing, such as every other number:
+
+        >>> iterable = [2, 4, 6, 10, 12]
+        >>> for group in consecutive_groups(iterable, step=2):
+        ...     print(list(group))

+    A negative *step* finds descending runs:
+

+        ...     print(list(group))

+
     Each group of consecutive items is an iterator that shares its source with
     *iterable*. When an output group is advanced, the previous group is

         [[1, 2], [11, 12], [21, 22]]
 
     """
+    # Validate eagerly, so a bad step is reported when the function is called
+    # rather than when the result is first iterated over.
+    if step == 0:
+        raise ValueError('step must not be zero')
+
     if ordering is None:
         key = lambda x: x[0] - x[1]
     else:

-    for k, g in groupby(enumerate(iterable), key=key):

+    def ret():
+        # Pair each item with a counter that advances by *step*. While each
+        # item's position advances by the same amount as the counter, the
+        # difference between the two (the key) stays the same; any other
+        # change in position changes the key and starts a new group.
+        for _, g in groupby(zip(count(0, step), iterable), key=key):

+    def test_step(self):
+        iterable = [2, 4, 6, 10, 12]
+        actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=2)]
+        expected = [[2, 4, 6], [10, 12]]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+        # Items that are only one apart are not adjacent when step=3
+        iterable = [0, 3, 6, 7, 8, 11, 20]
+        actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=3)]
+        expected = [[0, 3, 6], [7], [8, 11], [20]]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+    def test_step_positional(self):
+        iterable = [5, 10, 15, 16, 21]
+        actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, None, 5)]
+        expected = [[5, 10, 15], [16, 21]]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+    def test_step_custom_ordering(self):
+        iterable = 'acegjlnq'
+        actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, ord, 2)]
+        expected = [['a', 'c', 'e', 'g'], ['j', 'l', 'n'], ['q']]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+    def test_negative_step(self):
+        iterable = [9, 8, 7, 3, 2]
+        actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=-1)]
+        expected = [[9, 8, 7], [3, 2]]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+        iterable = [30, 20, 10, 11, 1, -9]
+        actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=-10)]
+        expected = [[30, 20, 10], [11, 1, -9]]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+        # Ascending runs are not grouped when the step is negative
+        iterable = [1, 2, 3]
+        actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=-1)]
+        expected = [[1], [2], [3]]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+    def test_negative_step_custom_ordering(self):
+        iterable = 'zyxvu'
+        actual = [
+            list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, ord, step=-1)
+        ]
+        expected = [['z', 'y', 'x'], ['v', 'u']]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+    def test_zero_step(self):
+        # The error is raised by the call itself, before any iteration
+        with self.assertRaises(ValueError):
+            mi.consecutive_groups([1, 2, 3], step=0)
+
+        with self.assertRaises(ValueError):
+            mi.consecutive_groups('abc', ord, 0)
+
+        with self.assertRaises(ValueError):
+            mi.consecutive_groups([], step=0)
+
+    def test_step_matches_pairwise_differences(self):
+        # Items are grouped together exactly when each one's position is
+        # *step* more than the position of the item before it.
+        rng = Random(0)
+        for step in (-3, -2, -1, 1, 2, 3):
+            for _ in range(50):
+                iterable = []
+                n = rng.randrange(-10, 10)
+                for _ in range(rng.randrange(12)):
+                    n += rng.choice([step, step, step, 1, -1, 0, 2 * step])
+                    iterable.append(n)
+
+                expected = []
+                for item in iterable:
+                    if expected and (item - expected[-1][-1] == step):
+                        expected[-1].append(item)
+                    else:
+                        expected.append([item])
+
+                with self.subTest(step=step, iterable=iterable):
+                    actual = [
+                        list(g)
+                        for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=step)
+                    ]
+                    self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+    def test_default_step(self):
+        # Leaving out step gives the same groups as the original algorithm
+        def original(iterable):
+            key = lambda x: x[0] - x[1]
+            for _, g in groupby(enumerate(iterable), key=key):
+                yield list(map(itemgetter(1), g))
+
+        rng = Random(1)
+        for _ in range(100):
+            iterable = [rng.randrange(-10, 10) for _ in range(12)]
+            if rng.random() < 0.5:
+                iterable.sort()
+            expected = list(original(iterable))
+            with self.subTest(iterable=iterable):
+                actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable)]
+                self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+                actual = [
+                    list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=1)
+                ]
+                self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+    def test_groups_share_source(self):
+        iterable = [1, 3, 5, 10, 12, 14]
+        all_groups = mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=2)
+
+        first_group = next(all_groups)
+        self.assertNotIsInstance(first_group, list)
+        self.assertIs(iter(first_group), first_group)
+        self.assertEqual(next(first_group), 1)
+
+        # Advancing to the next group consumes the rest of the previous one
+        second_group = next(all_groups)
+        self.assertEqual(list(first_group), [])
+        self.assertEqual(list(second_group), [10, 12, 14])
+        self.assertEqual(list(all_groups), [])
+
+    def test_lazy(self):
+        # Nothing is consumed until the output is iterated over
+        source = iter([1, 2, 3])
+        all_groups = mi.consecutive_groups(source, step=1)
+        self.assertEqual(next(source), 1)
+        self.assertEqual([list(g) for g in all_groups], [[2, 3]])
+

-def consecutive_groups(iterable, ordering=None):
+def _consecutive_runs(iterable, ordering, step):
+    'Lazily yield the groups for consecutive_groups(), given a non-zero step'
+    # Pair each item with its expected offset: 0, step, 2 * step, ...
+    # Within a run, every item sits exactly *step* past the one before it,
+    # so an item's expected offset minus its actual position stays the same
+    # for the whole run and serves as the groupby() key. A unit step pairs
+    # items with plain enumerate() indexes, exactly as before *step* existed.
+    if step == 1:
+        offsets_and_items = enumerate(iterable)
+    else:
+        offsets_and_items = zip(count(0, step), iterable)
+
+    if ordering is None:
+        key = lambda x: x[0] - x[1]
+    else:
+        key = lambda x: x[0] - ordering(x[1])
+
+    for k, g in groupby(offsets_and_items, key=key):
+        yield map(itemgetter(1), g)
+
+
+def consecutive_groups(iterable, ordering=None, *, step=1):

     returning their position.

         ['i']
         ['l', 'm', 'n', 'o', 'p']
 
+    Neighboring items are adjacent when the later one's position minus the
+    earlier one's equals *step*. The default *step* of ``1`` finds runs that
+    count up by one; other values find runs with a different spacing:
+
+        >>> iterable = [2, 4, 6, 10, 12, 13]
+        >>> for group in consecutive_groups(iterable, step=2):
+        ...     print(list(group))
+        [2, 4, 6]
+        [10, 12]
+        [13]
+
+    A negative *step* finds runs that count down:
+
+        >>> iterable = [9, 8, 7, 3, 2]
+        >>> for group in consecutive_groups(iterable, step=-1):
+        ...     print(list(group))
+        [9, 8, 7]
+        [3, 2]
+
+    *step* can only be passed by keyword. A *step* of ``0`` raises
+    ``ValueError`` as soon as :func:`consecutive_groups` is called, before
+    anything is read from *iterable*.
+
     Each group of consecutive items is an iterator that shares its source with
     *iterable*. When an output group is advanced, the previous group is
     no longer available unless its elements are copied (e.g., into a ``list``).

+    def test_step_spacing(self):
+        # A gap that is a multiple of the step still starts a new group
+        iterable = [0, 3, 6, 12, 15, 16, 19]
+        actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=3)]
+        expected = [[0, 3, 6], [12, 15], [16, 19]]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+    def test_negative_step(self):
+        for step, iterable, expected in [
+            (-1, [9, 8, 7, 3, 2], [[9, 8, 7], [3, 2]]),
+            (-1, [1, 2, 3], [[1], [2], [3]]),
+            (-2, [10, 8, 6, 5, 3, 4], [[10, 8, 6], [5, 3], [4]]),
+        ]:
+            with self.subTest(step=step, iterable=iterable):
+                groups = mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=step)
+                self.assertEqual([list(g) for g in groups], expected)
+
+    def test_step_with_ordering(self):
+        # ordering is still the second positional argument
+        groups = mi.consecutive_groups('acegjlmzyx', ord, step=2)
+        actual = [''.join(g) for g in groups]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, ['aceg', 'jl', 'm', 'z', 'y', 'x'])
+
+        groups = mi.consecutive_groups('zyxvu', ordering=ord, step=-1)
+        actual = [''.join(g) for g in groups]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, ['zyx', 'vu'])
+
+    def test_unit_step(self):
+        iterable = [-10, -8, -7, -6, 1, 2, 4, 5, -1, 7]
+        expected = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable)]
+        actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=1)]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+    def test_zero_step(self):
+        for step in (0, 0.0):
+            with self.subTest(step=step):
+                iterator = iter([1, 2, 3])
+                # Raised by the call itself, before anything is read
+                with self.assertRaises(ValueError):
+                    mi.consecutive_groups(iterator, step=step)
+                self.assertEqual(next(iterator), 1)
+
+    def test_lazy_groups(self):
+        # Each group is an iterator over a shared, possibly infinite, source
+        groups = mi.consecutive_groups(count(0, 5), step=5)
+        first = next(groups)
+        self.assertIsInstance(first, Iterator)
+        self.assertEqual(list(islice(first, 4)), [0, 5, 10, 15])
+
+    def test_shared_source(self):
+        groups = mi.consecutive_groups([1, 3, 5, 10, 12, 20], step=2)
+        first = next(groups)
+        self.assertEqual(next(first), 1)
+        second = next(groups)
+        # Advancing to the next group leaves the previous one exhausted
+        self.assertEqual(list(first), [])
+        self.assertEqual(list(second), [10, 12])
+        self.assertEqual([list(g) for g in groups], [[20]])

+        expected = [[9, 8, 7], [3, 2]]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+        # Increasing items aren't grouped when the step is negative
+        iterable = [1, 2, 3]
+        actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=-1)]
+        expected = [[1], [2], [3]]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+    def test_fractional_step(self):
+        iterable = [Fraction(1, 3), Fraction(2, 3), 1, 2, Fraction(7, 3)]
+        step = Fraction(1, 3)
+        actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=step)]
+        expected = [[Fraction(1, 3), Fraction(2, 3), 1], [2, Fraction(7, 3)]]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+    def test_lazy(self):
+        for iterable, step in [
+            ([1, 2, 3, 10, 11], 1),
+            ([2, 4, 6, 10, 12], 2),
+        ]:
+            with self.subTest(step=step):
+                # Nothing is consumed until iteration starts
+                it = iter(iterable)
+                mi.consecutive_groups(it, step=step)
+                self.assertEqual(list(it), iterable)
+
+                # Groups are iterators that share the source, so advancing
+                # to the next group exhausts the previous one
+                groups = mi.consecutive_groups(iter(iterable), step=step)
+                first = next(groups)
+                self.assertEqual(next(first), iterable[0])
+                second = next(groups)
+                self.assertEqual(list(first), [])
+                self.assertEqual(list(second), iterable[3:])
+

+    def test_step(self):
+        iterable = [2, 4, 6, 10, 12, 15, 17, 20]
+        actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=2)]
+        expected = [[2, 4, 6], [10, 12], [15, 17], [20]]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+    def test_step_with_ordering(self):
+        iterable = 'acegjlmo'
+        actual = [
+            list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, ord, step=2)
+        ]
+        expected = [['a', 'c', 'e', 'g'], ['j', 'l'], ['m', 'o']]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+    def test_negative_step(self):
+        iterable = [9, 8, 7, 3, 2]
+        actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=-1)]
+        expected = [[9, 8, 7], [3, 2]]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+        # Increasing items aren't grouped when the step is negative
+        iterable = [1, 2, 3]
+        actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=-1)]
+        expected = [[1], [2], [3]]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+    def test_zero_step(self):
+        for step in (0, 0.0, Fraction(0)):
+            with self.subTest(step=step):
+                # Raised when called, without needing to iterate
+                with self.assertRaises(ValueError):
+                    mi.consecutive_groups([1, 1, 2], step=step)
+
+    def test_fractional_step(self):
+        iterable = [Fraction(1, 3), Fraction(2, 3), 1, 2, Fraction(7, 3)]
+        step = Fraction(1, 3)
+        actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=step)]
+        expected = [[Fraction(1, 3), Fraction(2, 3), 1], [2, Fraction(7, 3)]]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+    def test_float_step(self):
+        # Float error in step * index doesn't split runs
+        iterable = [0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.5, 0.6]
+        actual = [list(g) for g in mi.consecutive_groups(iterable, step=0.1)]
+        expected = [[0.1, 0.2, 0.3], [0.5, 0.6]]
+        self.assertEqual(actual, expected)
+
+    def test_lazy(self):
+        for iterable, step in [
+            ([1, 2, 3, 10, 11], 1),
+            ([2, 4, 6, 10, 12], 2),
+        ]:
+            with self.subTest(step=step):
+                # Nothing is consumed until iteration starts
+                it = iter(iterable)
+                mi.consecutive_groups(it, step=step)
+                self.assertEqual(list(it), iterable)
+
+                # Groups are iterators that share the source, so advancing
+                # to the next group exhausts the previous one
+                groups = mi.consecutive_groups(iter(iterable), step=step)
+                first = next(groups)
+                self.assertEqual(next(first), iterable[0])
+                second = next(groups)
+                self.assertEqual(list(first), [])
+                self.assertEqual(list(second), iterable[3:])
+
